Affini Domestication Guide

by Holly Adder (Witch_Freya)

Summary

A human rescued from the wreckage of a jump drive disaster exhibits the same biorhythms as an affini. Naturally, it results in answering the age-old question: Can someone reach the end of an affini wardship without becoming a floret?

The answer may surprise you. Set in @GlitchyRobo's Human Domestication Guide universe.

Notes

Hey all! If you like this work, consider checking out the pinned post on my Bluesky (1x1.adder.industries) to find information about others like it—including an edited and lightly reworked version of this story available for e-readers.

Chapter 1: In Which Elize is Genre Savvy

20 August, 2556 (Friday): 7 Cycles Post-Rescue

It was rare that a human’s first experience with the Affini Compact was a warship. Under most circumstances, humanity would never need to see something intended for combat at all. There was no reason; as a rule, they weren’t a threat. Few sophonts were, when it came to the gentle-but-stern embrace of the Compact.

Vervain Aphora, Second Bloom, considered this to be unfortunate. The Lycoridae was a masterpiece. Certainly, she was not the largest warship, nor the fastest, nor the strongest, but she was beautiful. What was the point in strength if it came at the cost of loveliness?

It was her understanding that this particular ship had been designed based on a Terran flower; the Stargazer lily. Rich pink blossomed out from the center into bright spots that eventually faded to stark, alabaster edges sharp enough to cut open a moon. Not that they ever did; that would be incredibly insensitive.

No, a shuttle was a much more effective of retrieving the human who had, somehow, ended up a far too close to the galactic hyperspace gate for comfort; crashed in the wreckage of a tin can—correction, an ‘escape shuttle’.

Vervain wondered idly at what size in Terran culture a can became a shuttle, since the two looked almost identical based on the imagery she could find. She’d have to make a note to ask.

According to the missive, the crash had been a week ago. Prior to Vervain’s arrival aboard the Lycoridae. Prior to the befuddlement of Astrae Zonale, First Bloom, who thought the Terran was just adorable and had been looking for her first floret but found herself at a loss when meeting with her. Prior to the nonsensical communication that Vervain received about a Terran who “felt dirty” to be around and who left poor Astrae’s biorhythms all out of wack.

So, here she was; the closest expert on affini resonance to the system. There certainly were those closer to the Core Worlds who knew more; those much older than she, those who would be fascinated by this problem, but Vervain called dibs and light-speed delay was her ally.

Mx. Zonale had been unharmed. After she’d made polite excuses to depart the human’s general area, she’d recovered fairly quickly. Everything had been right as rain by the time Vervain examined her.

“I feel fine! I’m not sure what came over me,” she had insisted. “It’s just… dirt and dust, something about that human felt wrong. I’m sorry, does that make me a bad person? I shouldn’t say such things, I’m sure she’s just precious, I just, y’know?”

Vervain was not certain that she did know, but she did keep careful notes of everything that Mx. Zonale shared with her before sending her away with a clean bill of health.

The more interesting conversation was yet to come. According to the readings, something had happened to Mx. Zonale’s resonance during her conversation with the human. The general-purpose sensors in the vicinity hadn’t been enough to record precisely what had happened, so Vervain would have to find out herself. It wouldn’t be the first time she’d used herself as a lab horse.

The human hadn’t been very forthcoming with information, it seemed. Since after Mx. Zonale had left her alone, no one else had been in the room with her, and she had jeered at the attempts at remote communication.

Vervain watched one of the recordings. In it sat the subject of all their curiosities; an ostensibly average human. One of the affini monitoring her had left some notes.

I’m now responsible for her, eeee!!! If I wasn’t a one-floret kinda flower, I’d totally be calling dibs. I just wish I coulda been the one to ask her name. :::( No fair! But I mean, just look at her!

Like, this absolutely adorable hairstyle! She won’t tell us what it’s called, but I did some digging and I thiiiiink it’s called a wolf cut! :::) Apparently wolves are a creature native to Terra a long, long time ago!!!

I don’t thiiiiink they were purple on the underside, but this little human decided she wanted her hair to be! If you ask me, the brown complements the topside really nicely!

… the notes went on like that for a while. Vervain skipped back to the intake recording from after the Terran had first woken up. Hopefully it wouldn’t be quite so… much.

“Hey cutie! Would you mind terribly if we got your name and pronouns for the register? It’d be just darling if we coul—”

The speaker was abruptly interrupted. “Could you cut it out with the saccharine shit? You sound like an infant—come ask me my name to my face, cutie.”

This recording was far from realtime, but Vervain startled, taken aback by the response. She could tell that the affini on the recording had been surprised too. “I—Well, I could just write down Cutie, Cute/Cutes/Cuteself, if you like!”

“Oh my god, put a bullet in me instead. Just fuckin’ put down Zee, she/her. Is this your method of torture? I think I’d rather go back down to the moon, suffocation seems easier.” Zee’s voice was strangely resonant and soothing; Vervain realized she’d stopped walking and had just settled on watching the recording. She rustled in irritation and kicked herself back into moving.

“Well, we can’t have that! Would you like anything t—”

Zee glared directly at the camera, made an obscene gesture with her hands, and then laid face down on the bed.

Elize was pissed. First, she woke up suffocating (which sucks, by the way). Then she got picked up by weeds (which suck, by the way). Then, she started to remember (which extremely sucks, by the way).

What sucked the most, though, was her former Captain. She was really, really glad she’d been within hearing range when he gave the absolutely insane order to spool up the Jump Drive before it had fully cooled. Sure, they were about to get got by pirates, but fuck, dude, it’s spaceflight 101! Don’t superheat yourself into hypermetric oblivion!

So yeah, what’s a bitch to do when she knows there’s less than a minute to escape certain doom? Batten down the hatches in an escape pod set to launch. She didn’t quite get away, but, well, perhaps the universe is benevolent after all.

Or perhaps something from between the fabric of reality decided it really wanted to hang out with a human.

‘I use she/her pronouns.’

‘Hush, darlin’, Daddy’s monologuing.’

‘… you don’t have to call me Poppy, but please don’t call me that.’

’What else am I supposed to do? I’m stuck in this cell until weeds decide to delete my sense of sapience, and likely you along with it. Care to blip us back to hyperspace or something?’

‘Believe it or not, entering hyperspace without any form of shuttle tends to be quite lethal. So, no.’

Yeah, so; as it turns out, if something vaguely god-like decides that your delicious, sculpted, spacer body is shaped more like ‘vehicle’ than ‘person’, they might just save you from oblivion! Only to yeet you right into suffocating.

‘I did apologize.’

’Not enough.’

’I kept the First Bloom from giving you a good home, despite the fact that she seemed very genuine. What more do you want from me?’

The First Bloom would be Astrae something-or-other; she couldn’t keep track. The one with the pretty blue eyes who found it hard to walk when Poppy—‘Thank you.’—worked its magic a bit—’Science, technically.’

’Could you let me finish?’

’Sorry.’

So yeah, now Elize had universe-enforced plurality. Or the event had just traumatized her so much that she’d finally cracked and had a traumagenic alter, like her ex-girlfriends would say. ’That’s a little insensitive to plurality.’

’Yeah, you’re right. Sorry; not exactly coping well with everything right now.’

’You know, perhaps an owner would help wi—’

’How about you keep this one from getting in my head? Maybe she’ll even slip up, and we can get out of here.’

’I can try… You don’t mind that I’m having to make some modifications to your body for this to work, right?’

’Eh, it’s always felt like a rental. Put a few holes in the wall, and we’ll patch it up before the landlord notices.’

’What the dirt is a landlord?’

’Nevermind.’

The painting on the wall abruptly shifted colors to reveal her next target—sorry, ‘caretaker.’

… Honestly, the affini looked like a disaster enough on her own without Elize’s help. Stray leaves couldn’t seem to choose a particular direction to point littered her forearms in mottled greens and yellows. Grass and greenery bloomed into a facsimile of hair that she had… was that metal? Did she have a metal ring in her hair? That felt wrong, somehow.

Honestly, though, the face was doing something for Elize. She had glasses. Elize had always been a bit of a sucker for glasses, and while they seemed totally impractical for an affini, well… she had to admit, they were kinda cute. Hell, she had dark circles under those pretty pink eyes of hers. Elize was a sap for a tired-looking woman.

Weirdest of all, she wore what looked like a Terran lab coat around her body.

That was intentional, right? It had to be. No part of an affini was accidental; not when it so perfectly replicated another sophont form. Nah, the disaster on the other side of the protective glass was a cultivated appearance. She had to have chosen it intentionally. She wanted to look like a human researcher. An exhausted one.

Zee wolf-whistled.

Vervain wasn’t quite certain what that noise meant, but at least she had her ward’s attention.

“Hello Zee! I am Vervain Aphora, Second Bloom. It’s a pleasure to meet you,” Vervain announced over the speakers. Unsurprisingly, Zee maintained her status quo of raising a middle finger to the camera before returning to the odd thing she was doing with her body. The ship helpfully informed her that it was called ‘yoga’.

“I know you don’t really want to talk, but if I maybe came in to say hi, do you think you could show me whatever you did to Mx. Zonale? I know it’s a lot to ask, but it’d be extremely helpful.” Vervain knew that this wasn’t strictly the proper way to speak to a feral, but everything else seemed to have failed thus-far.

Miraculously, Zee paused and looked up. “That’s the one who came in the other day?”

It took Vervain a moment to respond, not having anticipated to speak just yet. “Ah, yes! Astrae Zonale, First Bloom. You really did hurt her feelings, you know.”

“You’ve got me in a gilded cage, I think my feelings are the ones allowed to be hurt. At least the food’s good. Acoustics leave a bit to be desired.” Zee paused, and to Vervain’s surprise, she sang a musical scale.

Vervain froze, listening. The sounds bounced around the room, crisp and clean. Zee had an exceptional voice, even with just that little sample. It wasn’t until the last note had completely faded that Vervain spoke again.

“I could arrange to have the hab adjusted, if you’d prefer. It’s a smidge more restricted than usual, but sound should be fine. I’ll happily do that if you’d be willing to help me out, too.”

Zee seemed to consider it for a moment. “Fine, why not. I’m bored and you sound mostly genuine. If you call me ‘cutie’, though, all bets are off.”

Vervain let herself in.

The containment hab was spartan by affini standards. Minimal decorations, only like three board games, and not even a single ice cream cone in the freezer. Vervain would have to make a note to change that; even if Zee wouldn’t help her, it was terribly inhumane to withhold such things.

Zee sat up and fixed Vervain with a stare that made her halt in the middle of a step. Her eyes were gorgeous. A bright, startling green that seemed to shimmer in the light, browns and little flecks of blue, even. She felt like she was looking into the ship’s garden, not the eyes of a pet-to-be.

Those eyes scanned Vervain up and down in a motion that felt almost predatory in a way that made absolutely no sense. “Evenin’, doc. Or morning. Whatever you prefer, not like it matters,” she greeted.

Vervain addressed her nerves by prodding three carefully cultivated blooms in her chest to life. One would monitor and record her own biorhythm. One would do the same for other rhythms in the room. The last kept an eye on the intersection; something she specialized in. The way that two rhythms met and harmonized; the social polyrhythm of two or more affini getting on well. Simply tracking the individuals wasn’t enough for that. Every meeting had an element of perfect entropy that couldn’t be replicated elsewhere.

Good. As she anticipated, the only resonance in the room was her own. So, what had caused the problem with Astrae?

“It’s closer to morning, honestly. Can I get you anything? I’d love to make sure you’re comfortable, so we can talk a bit, and you could maybe help me understand what happened,” Vervain chirped.

“Are the glasses real?” Zee interrogated, not answering her question. Rude. Although it was a question that she’d actually love to answer.

“Actually, yes! I always thought they were a cute look when I’ve run into various other Terrans, so I decided I’d have some of my own. Do you like them?”

“They make you look like a nerd,” Zee remarked, before snorting. “Yeah, honestly; it’s cute.”

Not the direction she was expecting this to go. Okay. Recalibrate, Vervain; you’re not used to ferals, but you can handle this.

She focused up, letting the patterns flowing through her essence push outward into a stronger force and encroaching on Zee’s space a bit further. For a moment, the girl seemed actually surprised. Perhaps she could start getting some answers.

“Thank you, darling. Could you maybe tell me about Astrae now? I’d love to hear what happened there,” Vervain pushed. The affini were a cognitohazard. Vervain was a cognitohazard. She could do this.

She gently set her fingers beneath Zee’s chin and guided the human’s eyes up to meet her own.

Zee allowed the touch. Her eyes locked on the affini.

Vervain balked.

There was no surrender there, no hesitation; none of the bliss she’d expected to see from someone playing along to her patterns. No. Zee was focused and defiant.

Vervain had made a mistake.

The other flowers in her chest began to resonate an impossibility; one that had been previously dormant springing to life and humming in a way that felt far too peaceful and scared her more than she had known possible.

“What… What are you doing?” Vervain whispered.

Zee’s voice sounded different. Beautiful, melodic, but not quite the same as before. “Shh, Vervain, right? S’okay, just relax, lil’flower.”

Zee was humming.

The resonance poured across Vervain’s body, an addictive sort of euphoria stringing out along her vines and working its way into her core. How? This wasn’t possible. No human could do this.

Zee could. The patterns cascaded across Vervain’s existence, familiar in sensation while alien in origin. They made her feel dizzy and fuzzy. They made her feel angry for having been touched on the chin so dismissively. Touched on the chin? It was hard to draw the distinction between bodies like this.

The affini moved her hand away, and Vervain was happy about this. She felt in control. She felt controlled. Something itched in the recesses of her mind.

She felt something she’d boxed up and tucked away in her consciousness snap loose and rear its ugly head.

All at once the control was unpleasant. She felt violated, disgusted with herself. She couldn’t let this happen again. Not like this. This wasn’t her, this wasn’t right.

Zee stood up, and Vervain wished she’d made this form larger than it was. She felt meek against the Terran before her.

It took all of Vervain’s strength to snap herself out of it.

She did something she wasn’t proud of.

She ran.

Chapter 2: In Which Vervain Meets a Monster

Vervain Aphora, Second Bloom, was actually quite familiar with panic attacks. She recognized the ripple of fear that ran across her extremities, and she could follow its waves all the way back to her core, nestled precious like a human heart in her chest.

Recognizing her panic was one thing. Easing it was another beast entirely.

She caressed a series of growths nestled behind her core to entreat their aid. Each hummed a different tune as it awoke. Together, they harmonized; a gentle thrumming pressed right against her vulnerability. Frantic pulsations gradually followed the harmony, as two metronomes might eventually join the same beat.

One eye peeked open. How far had she gone?

Not very, as it turned out. Vervain had removed herself from the containment hab, but she hadn’t made it much farther before her vines refused to support her gait and she’d collapsed. She gently prodded the eye into function. What could she see?

Green. Room. Observation. Observation room, right. Water flowed along the edge of the room in a gorgeous little creek that someone had taken the time to sculpt. She strained her vines into it now, letting the gentle current join her artificial rhythm.

The hab itself was a standard model split on one side. Where Vervain recovered now was the smaller of the two; designed ostensibly for affini comfort. Where Vervain might have expected a chair, given her proclivities, another affini had nestled a decorative trellis up against the observation controls. She suspected the culprit was whoever had made the notes.

While she was grateful she hadn’t been observed in this state, shouldn’t someone have seen her? Someone had been responsible for the monitoring room before she’d arrived; someone had left all those notes. Someone should have been back eventually.

Ah. Okay. She could think about other things now, not just the panic. That was enough.

One by one, she silenced the floral timekeepers against her core. Too fast and the panic would well up again.

As they quieted, she plucked a petal from each of the three flowers within her belly and outstretched a tendril to settle them on the interfaces of the rhythmometer she’d brought with her.

Playback. Sandboxed playback, something safe, something guarded, something that couldn’t scare her again.

“Little trellis!!! It is you!!!!”

No. Please, dirt and dust and dying stars, no.

A thousand vines tangled into her own, dipping underneath her coat, curling through her hair, sliding around her chest and very nearly knocking the petals away from the reader. Vervain withdrew from it before her attacker got any more cuddly.

“Please be careful, Camellia. This is sensitive equipment,” Vervain chided, although she suspected it wouldn’t do her much good.

“That’s the first thing you have to say to me, Lissa? C’mon cutie, I know you missed me.”

Vervain shifted herself to gaze upon the only person who could have left those notes and reacted this way to seeing her. Camellia Incubare, Seventh Bloom. Bane of her existence. A plague upon the Compact. A monstrosity.

Five brightly glowing yellow eyes blinked back at Vervain in utter delight, and Vervain felt her core-of-ice melt just a little. A whirl of inhuman vines spread out behind ser, braced against the floor in more of a heap than a coherent semblance of legs. Great pink flowers bloomed here and there in a pattern bordering-on random, were it not for Vervain’s familiarity. Shifting and whirling, they formed notes for a musical composition—likely one of Camellia’s own, if not ser floret’s.

“Hi, Cammie.”

“Oh, it is you! I’m so pleased, I’m so pleased!!!” Camellia announced, resonating along every point of contact in a way that was terribly distracting but, admittedly, whisked away the last of her remaining panic.

“Where’s—“

“Tilly? Running a lil’ errand for me, that’s all! It’ll be back soon enough, don’t you worry your pretty little petals! Oh goodness, I’ve missed you, I meant to be here when you arrived, I’m terribly sorry! Tilly just, you know how it is—or I suppose you don’t, you’ve never taken a floret, but—it just absolutely needed five more minutes in bed, and you know I can’t say no to it! I’m a weak woman, Lis, I really am.”

“You need to stop calling me that, I haven’t… I haven’t done that sort of thing this whole bloom, Cam. You know that,” Vervain insisted.

Camellia fixed her with an intense, scrutinizing gaze, and the pattern of ser flowers changed. “Not even a lil’ bit, pretty please?” It was Vervain’s song. The one that Camellia had composed for her.

Vervain sighed. She never could say no to this woman. “Fine. Just… Keep it down, okay? I have a job to do, and you have a floret now, you can’t treat me like…” Like se once had. Like Vervain missed terribly. Like Vervain wanted, but had not allowed herself to have since her first bloom came to an abrupt conclusion.

“Oh splendid!” Camellia exclaimed, tangling about Vervain once more. “And look at you! You’re so different; you look—“ Camellia pulled vines and leaves and all manner of growth away, giving Vervain a moment to breathe and recompose herself. “Like a human, of course! Wow, the face is so much like them! It just makes me want to—“

“Camellia.” Vervain’s tone turned icy.

“Right! Right, sorry,” Camellia abated, pulling serself back further. “Genuinely, I like the look. It’s a little… Odd? But I think it kinda suits you, Trellis. I’m glad to see you, it’s been far too long.”

“I needed some space after you signed the paperwork with Tilly, you know that.”

Camellia’s energy dampened, and Vervain felt like kicking herself for ruining the mood. “… I’m sorry, I didn’t even consider—Was the bloom enough time? Is it okay that I—“

“Yes, no, yes; it’s fine, Cam. I’m glad to see you, really. The bloom was enough time.” Vervain hoped it had been long enough that Camellia couldn’t see right through her like se used to.

The musician seemed to consider for a moment, before everything picked up again. It was like a weight had lifted off the room. “Thank the Garden! I’m so glad you’re here, have you seen her yet? I made absolutely super-duper certain that the relay reached you first, ’cuz I heard you were really into humanity now, and I wanted to make sure that you had first dibs!”

Vervain didn’t even want to take the time to process the fact that Camellia had (gently) manipulated her into being here.

Camellia lurched onto the actual trellis in the room.

No, that wasn’t fair. Lurched implied it was inelegant. Nothing se ever did was inelegant; with how se shifted serself, it felt like the room was the one who had lurched and se had stayed in the same spot. Perhaps the Lycoridae itself had adjusted so carefully as to send Camellia exactly where se needed to be.

“I have,” Vervain confessed, warily eying the viewport. Zee had settled back into her ‘yoga’ routine as if nothing had happened. Vervain shuddered.

’That didn’t feel good, Elize,’ claimed the nagging voice that now seemed to be a permanent resident in the head of Elize “Zee” Nightingale.

Elize could feel Poppy pressing up against her mind, letting some of those emotions out. It felt gross, but, well, she couldn’t disagree that the reaction the dorky-looking affini had exhibited distressed her a bit.

When the first affini, Astrae, had invaded her space, that’d been different. Elize and Poppy had done a bit of Pacific Rim nonsense, and Astrae had started to feel really weird, made polite excuses, and left.

With that one… ‘Vervain,’ Poppy interjected.

With Vervain, the affini had lost it. Sure, Elize was being kept here, but it wasn’t like she had many better options with her own people, and this wasn’t all bad so far. She’d just wanted Vervain to not try to mess with her head. Elize probably wouldn’t have even noticed the rhythms coming off of the affini without Poppy, but together, it was impossible to ignore.

Vervain had tried to push hers onto Elize. Elize and Poppy had defended themselves. That was it. That was all.

So, why did she feel so damn guilty about it?

’Because we could feel what it did to her.’

Yeah.

Yeah, she could. She could remember the sudden discordant note that scattered the rest of Vervain’s rhythm, feeling like a knife driven into Elize’s chest from the sheer panic that boiled off of the affini. Elize had convinced Poppy afterward to mute the amount of feedback she could get, but that didn’t erase the reminder that, because of Elize’s actions, another person had nearly torn herself apart with fear.

She settled into a plank, and determined that she would keep it until the screaming in her muscles drowned out the thought of anything else.

For once, Poppy agreed with her.

Vervain didn’t hate Camellia. She never had, and she probably never could. She just really wanted to.

The biorhythm specialist joined her former friend near the controls, and several tendrils unwound from the trellis to loop around Vervain’s waist and pull her closer. “You spoke with Astrae, right? Gave me the shivers,” Camellia announced, shuddering for emphasis. “Who’d have thought someone so adorable could be so spooky! Like, do you see what she’s doing now?”

Vervain had noticed, but was pointedly avoiding looking too closely. Unfortunately, that left her with few options of other things to look at, since she would rather not stare pathetically at Camellia either.

*Ding!*

Oh, thank the Compact.

Vervain scrambled away from the observation desk to review her own machine.

“Whatcha got, Trelly?” pestered her irritation-from-on-high. “Ooo, that’s neat. Some kinda…” Cam trailed off, having very little idea what se was looking at, but wanting to be involved.

“It was running an analysis on the biorhythms I’ve tracked with the petals,” Vervain mumbles, hoping she didn’t have to explain too much further in depth. She stared, silently, at the results. Vervain didn’t want to explain these. Vervain was incredibly grateful Camellia couldn’t read these.

It had marked the event with Zee as a dissonance point.

Dissonance Point @ 1a259.735-36

Bloom(Self): 
    DP-5 (Irregularity spike); 
    DP+20 (Collapse imminent, you're kinky, aren't you?)
Bloom(Survey): 
    DP-5 (Acquired signal 2); 
    DP+5 (Lost signal 1); DP+25 (Lost signal 2).
Bloom(Harmony): 
    No data.

No harmony. None? The foreign rhythm hadn’t even attempted to harmonize with hers, it had just… brushed her right off, and nearly taken her with it. She made a mental note to adjust the reaction to her self-monitor as well. At the time, referring to another biorhythm taking her over in such jovial tones had been a notion she would have been flustered over.

Now, the fact that she’d joked about it so easily made a leaf fall off.

“Oh!! Did you speak with this cutie already?? How did it go? Oh goodness, tell me everything—or, wait, no, I can just—!!” Camellia rambled, before messing with the interface.

The recording.

Dirt.

“No, don’t watch that, please,” Vervain tried, diving for the controls. Camellia covered the necessary buttons in about a solid foot of plant matter.

“Nuh-uh, I wanna seeeeee.”

She watched herself, feeling sick.

“Oh! Oh she talked to you? Oh goodness, that’s fantastic, we haven’t managed to get…” Camellia trailed off, watching the interaction. “She’s right, you know—the glasses are cute, if a lil’ odd. Very Terran.”

Vervain chose not to respond. She wasn’t sure she could. She knew what would happen next, and that she couldn’t stop it from happening. She steeled herself against it instead. She wouldn’t panic again; it’d taken her off-guard, and she was better now.

She just didn’t want Camellia to see her like that.

“Ooo, putting on the charm, huh? This’ll be good.”

Vervain looked away.

Camellia’s rhythm resonated as clearly with Vervain as it ever had. She could feel the moment of surprise, then confusion, then worry, and then a lot of worry as a couple of tendrils reached out toward Vervain automatically.

“Vervain? What happened there? Are you okay?” Ser tone was different. Se’d even used the right name. A tendril brushed across her arm and Vervain retracted it promptly, fiddling with her machine to examine a few of the interesting points.

Zee was fascinating, after all. Terrifying, and fascinating.

“I’m fine,” Vervain lied. “Don’t worry about it.”

A tendril curled around her wrist and tugged. “No you’re not. I know you better than that, talk to me. What happened in there, Lissa?”

“I said I’m fine, Camellia.” Vervain tugged herself from the touch again, but Camellia encroached on her space again. Se kept getting closer, leaving ser seating trellis behind to push against Vervain’s boundaries.

“Talk to me, Trel.” Camellia pushed. Not physically, no. She could feel the rhythm, Camellia’s influence invading her space. This was a familiar one. One she knew. One she’d missed terribly, one she wanted to go back to. It wasn’t foreign, like Zee’s had been.

But this was the one that had made her react the way she had to the Terran.

Fear boiled within Vervain. She wouldn’t panic. Not in front of Camellia, not when it would mean falling apart like that. No. She had a better option than panicking.

She bristled, slapping away the tendrils and turning abruptly to the monstrous woman occupying a solid half of the observation hab.

“Dirt and dust, Camellia! Stop treating me like I’m your floret, you don’t get to do that anymore!”

Camellia snapped back ser vines as if se had been burned.

“I’m Maestrix’s floret, Miss!” announced a clammering, insectoid voice that Vervain had hoped never to hear again.

Tilly stood in the entrance, clutching something bundled against its chest and looking confused. Vervain retreated into herself, hardening her gaze and rearranging the pattern of her face to form a smile that appeared genuine, by any human standard. Her rhythms were all wrong though, and she knew ‘Maestrix’ Camellia Incubare, Seventh Bloom, could tell.

“Of course you are! There’s my precious instrument~” Camellia cooed, surging forward and tangling serself about the insectoid carapace of ser floret. Even with the sudden distraction, Vervain could tell that Camellia was hurt.

Yeah, well. Vervain was hurt.

Tilly had completed its task! It clutched the little music box close to its chest and vibrated with happiness, chittering all the while. The melody was far sadder than it would have liked, but Maestrix had asked for this particular tune, and it would never disappoint its affini!

It gently bundled the gift up once, twice, thrice in cloth. No harm would come to the gift, Maestrix wanted to impress an old friend and Tilly was very good at making small, pretty little music things.

Tilly savored the burst of reward chemicals it had been promised on completion of its task. They made it want to curl up at home, not go somewhere. But it had another task! One which promised more, and which would reunite it with its Maestrix! So, Tilly would make sure to bring the box.

It took a moment to admire the view from the area where it had been working. The great open expanse in the center of the Lycoridae was remarkable, wildlife blooming anywhere and everywhere it looked. How could it not be inspired by surroundings like these?

It set off, footfalls making little *click* noises as it walked. It liked the way the sounds felt on its body, like sharp percussion beats everywhere it walked.

The containment hab would not be far. It could meet Maestrix’s friend soon! Maestrix had talked very much about Doctor Vervain in the last few days, but before that, it had never heard of her before. Tilly was very curious what kind of affini might be so important and so unimportant at the same time.

Tilly arrived quickly enough, and it could feel something was wrong. Maestrix was worried. Tilly assumed the worry was about it, and promptly pushed the door open to announce happily that it had returned!

“Stop treating me like I’m your floret, you don’t get to do that anymore!” boomed from the other affini in the room. Tilly felt the way that Maestrix’s emotions shifted from worry to hurt, and some of that hurt felt like it came from Tilly itself.

Tilly set the bundle it had been carrying down on a little table by the door, before raising its voice:

“I’m Maestrix’s floret, Miss!”

Vervain was jealous of a bug.

What an awful thought to have.

Every part of it felt wrong against her mind. Jealousy? Unfair. Bad.

Bug? No. Rude. Tilly did nothing to her.

Tilly took away Camellia, of course it had done something to her.

That was bound to happen at some point anyway, Vervain had known it could never last.

Vervain brought herself up to her full height (which is to say, the minuscule height of the form she’d chosen). It was more of a matter of presentation and posture than power. “Mx Incubare, I’m going to need some time to study the feral. While I appreciate your monitoring so far, it’s no longer required—if you’d please leave, I’d like to be alone.”

Tilly looked well and truly baffled, while its owner simply composed serself back together and chimed an agreement. “Of course, Doctor!! Thank you so much for your help, it’s a delight to have you here!”

Vervain could tell it was just a façade. She’d wounded Camellia. Se was just doing what se did best; putting up a beautiful front. That didn’t mean Vervain would stop ser from leaving.

“The gift, Maestrix!” Tilly reminded, reaching for the bundle it had set down. Camellia curled a tendril around the outstretched arm.

“It can stay there, sweetness. Let’s go home, yes? The Doctor would like some privacy.”

Vervain watched as Tilly’s eyes glazed over, something from its implant clearly superseding coherent thought.

Jealous of a bug.

“Take care, Vervain. And… I’m here, if you want to talk,” Camellia murmured.

Vervain refused to look away from her rhythmometer until she heard the door whirr shut behind her.

Chapter 3: In Which Elize Tries Not to Care

23 August, 2556 (Monday): 10 Cycles Post-Rescue

For two days, Vervain was a deplorable excuse for an affini.

She did not do her work. Sure, she made certain that Zee was fed, watered, that she had the things she would need, that she was monitored, but it was a distraction to avoid the terrifying thing that Tilly had placed beside the doorway.

Everything she would need was contained within the monitoring area. As long as she didn’t leave, she wouldn’t need to touch the bundle on the table.

One single vine broke from the mostly-human form that Vervain Aphora, Second Bloom, had crafted as her own. It groped, blindly, for the package that had been left behind. For a moment, Vervain was tempted to shatter it upon the ground. She wished to make it as broken as she was.

But no; she could restrain herself. Unlike Camellia, she actually cared when she left something broken behind.

Again, that wasn’t right. She was being cruel. Even if they were just her thoughts, it still felt wrong to think of Camellia that way.

Vervain had been hurt, but she hadn’t even given Camellia the chance to comfort her. She’d left. Far, far away, where she wouldn’t have to confront the pain that harbored in her.

Rather than smash the bundle, she twisted vines under the fabric and carefully revealed its contents.

It really was beautiful.

Rich, lavender wood had been lovingly wrought by hand, warped and carved into a nearly perfect form. There were flaws, of course; anything made outside a fabricator would have flaws, but that was what made it hurt more. Tilly and Camellia had made this. Personally. For her.

She knew what it would play before she even opened it.

She wound it, and it wounded her.

Dextrous fingers that she’d spent far too many evenings designing just right eased the clasp loose and let free the music hiding within. The first notes brought back far too many memories.

A lifetime ago. Lissa Aphora, First Bloom, lay in a crumbled mass of greenery within the habitation unit of Camellia Incubare, Seventh Bloom. She would one day come to be known as Vervain, but first contact with humanity would not be for years yet.

In fact, the cotyledon program for the quinoth was in full swing. Lissa possessed a hab of her own, but she had not returned to it for quite some time. She had even moved much of her equipment here, so she could be herself in safety and privacy, but continue her work with the quinoth.

Within the safety of Maestrix’s hab she remained, where she could be someone else entirely.

While the name she wore was inspired by her surrender to her Maestrix, she would not wear the true, full name outside their safe place. Here, though? With her Maestrix’s tune gently playing in overwhelming little taps against her core? Trellis, First Floret, was safe.

“How are you doing, little instrument?” rumbled the biomass that was almost indistinguishable from her own with how tangled the two were. Braids of ser vines wove with Trellis’, flowers kissed flowers, and the rich aroma of their greenery clouded their shared space to the point of intoxication.

It would one day be said that humanity had a particularly high bonding instinct. For now, though, Trellis was quite certain that hers was stronger. She could not imagine a life without her Maestrix, and for a time yet, her Maestrix could not imagine a life without her.

“Mmmgood, Maestrix,” rumbled the resonance-drunk affini. Her own rhythm was so quiet as to be almost invisible, harmonizing and surrendering to the masterful composition of the most incredible composer in the universe.

Trellis felt the gentle caresses against her core change; teasing taps shifting to longer notes. The blooms upon her Maestrix’s body took up a different pattern. “Good. I wrote something for you, floret. I’d like very much if you could pay attention from me, could you do that?”

She could. She could do anything with instruction.

Her Maestrix strained, various vines and tendrils vanishing into well-placed grooves around the hab. One after another, sounds flowed into their space. She knew that Camellia was making them; instruments and tools hidden away out of sight, but in her current state it felt like magic.

She recognized the rhythm of the music. It was theirs. It was her Maestrix’s specialty, something that se’d only developed as a result of knowing Trellis. Trellis taught her Maestrix to look for the harmony of two affini rhythms.

Her Maestrix took them and made them into something else entirely.

For that ephemeral infinity, there was nothing but the perfect music of Trellis and her Maestrix.

Elize held the infuriatingly comfortable pillow against her face, wishing to any god who would listen that it would shut out the noise. Fuck, she’d settle for wishing to an affini, but the one in her head certainly didn’t seem to want to do anything about it.

’I feel it just as you do, Elize. Were I a bloom, Lycoridae Actual would already be putting a vote forth to intervene. An affini distressing a floret-to-b—’

“Poppy…” Elize growled, aloud for once. She was having a hard time focusing, and she could tell that her monitor wasn’t precisely in the right state to be listening terribly close to what she said.

’Sigh. Fine. An affini distressing an independent in this way is hardly in-line with the Treaty. Any Treaty, in fact.’

’Did you just say ’sigh?’ Ugh. I’m not distressed, I’m just irritated.’

’That’s not really all that different, you know.’

Elize wanted to throw the pillow at the voice in her head, but she imagined she’d probably set off some kind of warning if she looked like she was hurting herself.

For the last two days, the feeling of misery from the dorky flower that she’d messed up had continued to amplify. Poppy had been surprised that Elize could even feel it.

’The containment hab should prevent that sort of cross-contamination. I have felt my people across the void of space and between the folds of reality, but you? You’re human. A very odd human, but human nonetheless.’

’As you’ve made abundantly clear, thank you. I take it there aren’t that many case studies of someone like you descending to slum it with a lowlife like me, eh? I’m honored, your divineliness,’ Elize bantered. She knew that Poppy wasn’t too keen on being referred to as a deity, even a former one, but it was too ridiculous a notion to not poke fun of at least a little. She also felt the telltale sting of disappointment over her own self-deprecation, even if it was intended as comedic.

For the last two days, the feeling of misery had grown stronger, yes. But for the last two minutes, it was overwhelming. She could feel the pain resonating off the affini in the next area over like it was a slap to the face. Like there were flames licking at the edges of her vines—her fingertips.

’Please say something, blossom. Even if it’s just telling her to leave you alone, you know as well as I do it’s not going to get any better.’ Poppy was infuriatingly right and Elize was just an obtuse ass, but at this point it was almost a matter of pride. How much could she endure? This was torture, sure, but she hadn’t said anything so far and if she said something now, it would mean admitting defeat.

’Why do you want to be tortured so badly?’

’Maybe that’s your urge, little goddess.’

Huh. The feeling of something blooming across her body was a surreal one, but it definitely coded to ‘flustered.’ Direct hit. She savored her victory for all of ten seconds, before feeling a wave of ‘sad’ cascade over her.

… ah shit.

Vervain was crying.

Fine! That’s enough.

But she was gonna have an ice cream cone first. They were delicious.

The music box quieted. It wasn’t perfect. Of course it wasn’t; it would be impossible to replicate all of the various nuances of the music that Camellia had written for Trell—for Vervain. But it was perfectly painful, in a way she hadn’t remembered she could hurt.

In some small part of her, Vervain knew that this was not a blade, but an embrace. It was meant to be a gift, something to show her that—while they were different people now—Camellia had not forgotten her. Yet, it felt like flames, licking at her leaves and threatening to incinerate her entirely.

She knew that the ship’s Firebreak would snap such things out of reality faster than she could think, but the fire burnt her nonetheless.

She curled up onto the chair she had fabricated and hugged her knees to her chest, letting the music box drop to the floor. Of course, she caught it before it hit the ground—a vine gently set it on the console instead, but it was one that held very little of her attention.

She pressed her nearly-human face into her knees and decided to try something she’d heard about humanity.

She gathered her moisture together and let capillary action take over, adding a dash of salination for the effect. Alongside her eyes, she gently perforated little grooves that would free the solution from where she’d mixed it.

Little teardrops traced down her face.

She had to admit, it felt good in a way.

More flowed freely, letting herself go. The tears ran wet and hot along her face as she adjusted even her breathing patterns to gasp and sniffle. It felt really good, like the pain in her core was flowing out through the motions and tears.

Vervain wasn’t sure how long she sat like that, bundled up as small as she could make herself on the chair. It didn’t feel long enough when it was interrupted.

There was a three-tone *tap, tap, tap* on the glass that separated her from her ward. She knew Zee couldn’t see through it—it was set for a single direction right now—but she still startled out of her state and felt like she made direct eye contact with the human on the other side.

How did Zee know exactly where she was sitting?

“Uh, hey, doc,” the human greeted. “Figure I’m the last son-of-a-bitch you wanna hear from right now, but like—do you wanna talk about it?”

What?

No. There was no way Zee could know. She double and triple checked the glass’s settings, then the audio transmitters, then everything else she could think of. Hell, she even confirmed that there wasn’t any airflow passing from her side of the hab to Zee’s. Nothing.

There was no way she could have known what kind of mess Vervain was.

So, Vervain composed herself. The tears she’d created recycled back into her reserves. She halted her breathing altogether, then resumed it at a normal cadence.

Once she was absolutely certain, she activated the intercom and made herself visible again. “Talk about what, darling?” she asked, in her best impression of Camellian enthusiasm.

“Whatever the other weed said to you that got you so fucked up, doc. Don’t totally know what it was, but, uh,” Zee ruffled fingers through her hair, setting a pattern of purple and brown dancing. She really was beautiful. She would make some lucky affini very, very happy someday. “Well, I can tell you’ve been struggling with it, and I don’t think you should be alone right now. Which sounds stupid since you can see me pretty much constantly, but, uh. Whatever. Besides the point.”

Vervain was a good affini. She could be good. This was her ward, she was responsible for her. She just had to figure out how she’d been observed, fix the problem, and never-ever talk about her own pain to anyone, especially not a future pet.

“I’m quite certain I don’t know what you’re talking about, blossom. I apologize that I haven’t been more active in getting you cozy, I’ve just been terribly busy reviewing what we learned the other day about you! We should be able to get you somewhere more comfortable within a day or so, and then we can see about adoption. Won’t that be love—“

“Cut the shit, doc,” Zee demanded. Throughout Vervain’s speech, the human’s face had progressively soured until she’d interrupted entirely. Vervain stalled, shocked by how… firm the order was. “You’ve been an utter mess for two days, and I’m tired of just letting it happen. Think of it this way—before long, I probably won’t even remember my own name, right? So, what’s the harm in getting a bit of capital-T Trauma off your chest to an incredibly bored listener?”

She really drove home how poorly Vervain had acted as a caretaker. Dirt.

“… That’s not… Strictly accurate, nor appropriate,” Vervain stammered, trying to get her footing in a conversation that had taken her by such storm. What would Camellia do? Or anyone, for that matter? Any affini who wasn’t quite so messed up as Vervain was. “I’m here to take care of your needs and make, uh, super-duper certain that you have—“

“I said cut the shit and talk to me, Vervain.”

From a few non-affini sophonts, Vervain had heard of the phenomena of hypermetric kick. She had never experienced it, of course—affini lacked the silly hollow spots in them that many sophonts possessed, and thus were typically unable to experience such things.

That said, from what she’d heard, the way Zee’s voice affected her now was quite analogous to that kind of kick.

A few hesitant blooms perked up and showed their petals along her arms, embarrassed by her own reaction. “Sorry. I’m just… Camellia used to mean something to me.”

Why are you telling her this, Vervain?

Dirt and dust, she made such an awful example of an affini.

“Go on,” Zee insisted. She snagged a chair to prop it closer to the window, then dropped into it and crossed her arms pointedly.

“… Se was, uh. A friend.”

Zee held up a finger. “You’re lying. Try again.”

What!?

“Se was! Camellia was my oldest friend, we were very close. That’s all.”

A second finger went up. Vervain balked, uncertain why she felt so much like she was being backed into a corner in this conversation. After a second, Zee put the second finger down. “Eh, on second thought, that’s half true, so I guess I’ll keep it at one lie for now. But that’s not the whole truth, is it? Se was a bit more than a friend.”

Vervain was surprised to hear the pronoun roll off Zee’s tongue so casually. Most Terrans required a bit more prompting to use something that wasn’t terribly common in their culture—se/ser existed, of course, but it was rare enough that most hadn’t heard of it. Moreover, she was surprised to hear herself called out so easily on her bluff.

Her façade cracked a bit. “How do you know…?”

Zee shrugged. “I can just tell. So, truth time. Let’s try to avoid lies, yeah?” she gestured to her raised pointer finger. “I’ll even give you one more as buffer space, although I’ll call you out on it. If you get to three, though, it’s game over.”

A few more flustered flowers bloomed on Vervain’s shoulders, but she nodded her agreement. She wasn’t sure what game over meant, but she had a feeling she would rather not find out.

“Lovely. Then, Camellia. Spill it; how’d se hurt you?”

Vervain averted her eyes. Zee’s were pretty and distracting, and she needed to try to compose herself to make a convincing falsehood. She wasn’t just representing herself right now, she represented the Compact as a whole. Showing that an affini could be weak and vulnerable to a Terran feralist absolutely could not be good for helping her fit into her future perfect pet role.

So, semi-truths.

“I loved Camellia very much, and for a long time se loved me too. Once se took ser floret, though, se didn’t really have time for me anymore, and I—unfairly—resented ser for it.”

Well done, Vervain. That was cleverly worded.

Zee seemed to consider it for a long moment, before nodding. “You’re still holding out on me, but that’s progress. Floret, yeah? That’s what y’all call, what, humans?”

Oh, an easy question. “No, not quite. It’s the name for any sophont species that has found its home with one of our kind. A sophont who will know nothing but safety and comfort for the rest of their life.”

“Ah. A pet. Got it,” Zee confirmed, although it seemed like this wasn’t new information to her. She hadn’t even flinched over the notion. Weren’t feralists supposed to be… aggressive? Difficult? Zee felt downright empathetic. This was weird. “So, why would Camellia adopting a pet mean she doesn’t have time for her friend?”

Red. Emergency. Abort. There was no half-truth in this part of the conversation, just an incredibly anxious affini feeling panic welling up in her, flashing back to the music box, to her fight, to her last bloom, to how she ended, to—“Hey! Vervain! Eyes on me, stay with me.”

Vervain met Zee’s gaze abruptly, feeling like she’d been struck. Zee’s eyes were soft. Gentle. What the dirt was going on? Why was she acting like this?

“Your rhythm, yeah? Ease it up a bit. Five long pulses, hold for two, then repeat.”

There was no way Zee should have this knowledge, but Vervain wasn’t in a state to disagree. Five long. They resonated throughout her, starting in her core and banishing the dissonant forces near the tips of her vines. Hold for two. Repeat.

The panic ebbed.

Zee seemed to relax too. Odd.

“Better?”

Vervain nodded. Zee’s eyes were really pretty.

“Yes. Thank you, cuti—um. Thank you, Zee.”

“Good catch. You’re welcome—and you can call me Elize, if you’d like.”

Vervain sat up. Was she actually getting something out of this Terran, despite all the mud she’d brought into the hab? “Elize. Thank you, Elize. How did you know I was…” she hesitated, trying to find the right words.

“Upset? I can feel it.” She set a hand against her chest. “Not to make you guilty or anything, but I could feel it for the last two days. Which, uh…” Elize grit her teeth. “Well, maybe the fact that I let it go on says more about me than it does about you. Sorry about that.”

“What do you mean, ‘you can feel it?’ That’s not possible; your room is on an isolated… everything.” Her subject just kept getting more and more interesting. She once again reviewed all of the settings (literally, all of them), and then confirmed with Lycoridae Actual that the systems in the room were functioning properly.

All of this happened before Elize responded.

“How about a little game? Secret for a secret. I’ll tell you something as long as you tell me something.” Elize grinned. The look made Vervain a little nervous, but it already seemed that Elize knew far too much, so… At least this way, she could do her job.

“Okay. Secret for a secret. You seem to know some of mine already, so perhaps you could tell me one of yours?” Vervain pushed her luck.

“Hm. Okay. Let’s make it interesting. Ever heard of ‘Two truths and a lie’?” Vervain hadn’t. “For the first round, I’ll make three statements about myself. You’ll have to guess which one is a lie. If you get it right, I take a shot.”

“A shot!?” Vervain exclaimed. “I have no interest in shooting you, little one! I don’t know what you’ve read, but no one in our care comes to any harm, I can assure you.”

Elize was laughing at her. Why was the human laughing? She was quite serious! This was important!

“Not a bullet, you dork. A shot! Like, alcohol! Booze. Liquor. The happy juice. Bad-decision water. Yeah?”

Oh.

Oh, Vervain felt quite silly.

“This feels as if it is weighted in your favor, since you seemingly can tell when I’m lying. I don’t feel as if it would be very responsible of me to be intoxicated while working.”

“So stop working. You’ve barely done anything for two days, what’s another hour or two?” Elize had a point, even if Vervain felt quite sheepish about her poor job performance. “I’ll tell Poppy to make things a little confusing, and it’ll be harder to tell which is true. Sound fair?”

“Who is Poppy?”

Elize tapped a slender, lovely finger against her nose. “Nuh-uh. Gotta agree to the game first, yeah? That’d be telling.”

On one hand, being affected while around Elize sounded like a very bad decision. On the other, this was her job. She could actually find out what had happened to Elize this way, perhaps get a little closer to solving the problem that she’d been called here to fix, and who the dust was ‘Poppy?’

Besides, she could cheat. She had access to all sorts of biometrics for the Terran; there would be clear indications of her telling a falsehood.

What’s the worst that could happen?

“Oh, very well. Two truths and a lie.”

“Lovely! I’ll start.”

Chapter 4: In Which Vervain Lies

Vervain had been given three options.

Each of them was startling on their own, but only one could be a lie? That didn’t seem possible somehow. Surely Elize was mistaken.

“One,” Elize had said. “I survived a Jump Drive explosion while in a defective escape pod.”

That was impossible. Vervain wasn’t very knowledgeable about humanity’s drives, but she did know that a rip in spacetime like that would mean certain death to everyone aboard. That had to be the lie. Surely.

But.

“Two. Poppy was a god, and now she’s my headmate.”

That sounded like a delusion. There were no such things as gods; the Compact was as close as any race would get. If gods existed outside their purview, then they were surely horrid sadists determined to make their own creations solve the barbarity they wrought in the universe. So, that was the lie.

“Three. I want to be a floret.”

The console helpfully notified her of falsehood. Various bodily functions lit up predictably; something that had happened during neither of the other two statements. That’s what she chose to lie about? The inevitable? Vervain had to admit, Elize was becoming more and more baffling to her by the moment.

“I know it’s three,” Vervain admitted. Elize grinned, grabbed the little glass off the table, and knocked it back. Admittedly, Vervain felt a little guilty for giving Elize actual alcohol. She consoled herself by mixing in a concoction of nutrients and transmission-capable microflora that would give her a better idea of what was going on biologically underneath the hood. At least this would be slightly beneficial.

“But neither of those first two things are possible. Explain,” Vervain insisted. Elize’s eyes were sharp and pointed and, for a moment, Vervain nearly whimpered out a ‘please.’ She restrained herself, barely.

“No.”

Vervain pouted.

“Your turn, MJ.”

Vervain rustled with confusion. “MJ?”

“Y’know, Mary-Jane. Like… weed.” There was a moment where Elize almost seemed sheepish about her joke, but Vervain’s hair abruptly raised, and she forced out the human equivalent of a laugh to project her emotion in a way Elize could interpret.

“I’m surprised I haven’t heard that one before. But… but you can’t really expect me to believe that you have a god in your head,” Vervain pushed.

“Is that your lie? Because you’re making this round really easy if all I have to do is tell you I do expect you to believe that,” Elize countered.

Dirt and dust. She really wasn’t getting anywhere with these questions. Fine, she’d play the game. Vervain poked at the console with irritation. Not a button that would do anything; there were quite a few buttons that did nothing, but simply felt good to press.

“Oh very well. One. I spent my first bloom aboard the Lycoridae. Two, I have never taken a floret. Three, I have enjoyed the time I’ve spent talking to you more than anyone else aboard.”

Elize winced. “That’s depressing, doc.”

“What? Why?”

“Because we’ve barely spoken,” Elize reminds her. Ah. Right. “But that one’s too much of a bummer to be a lie, sorry. So… I’d guess floret?” Something in Elize’s biometrics spiked, but Vervain was too distracted by a momentary victory to notice.

“Incorrect! Perhaps you cannot read my mind after all.”

Elize smiled, shrugged her shoulders, and sat back. “Told ya so.”

Vervain felt quite pleased with herself. Finally, she had the upper hand in their conversation. It had taken long enough. “Your turn, flower. Tell me your mistruths and your facts.”

Across the glass, her captive ward snorted and kicked up her feet on the table. She nearly knocked a shot to the floor in the process. Even if she had, it would have simply been brushed away to a hidden decompiler in the room and reabsorbed into the ship’s reserves. “A’ight. Yours were kinda boring and depressing, though. Make it a bit more interesting; otherwise I’ll make you take a shot anyway.”

“What?” Vervain bristled. She gently patted down her coat, letting some of the brambles that had started to prod through ease back into her form. “That wasn’t in the rules.”

“Yeah, well, we’re playing the Elize-Vervain special variant, consider yourself lucky.” Before Vervain could protest more, Elize continued. “One. Poppy is an affini. Two. I don’t believe humanity deserves to be domesticated. Three. I think your dorky reactions are actually kinda cute.”

“…” Vervain felt herself grind to a halt, as well as about a dozen blooms sprout across her lower torso out of view of the glass. Surely the last one was the lie. Right? Right. That would make sense. The first one was ridiculous, of course, but if she bought into the odd delusion that Elize seemed to have, it could, perhaps, stand to reason that she believed she had an affini god in her head. Two was an obvious truth.

Which left… “Three?”

Elize made an abrupt, throaty sound that Vervain recognized as a warning buzzer imitation. “Wrong. The correct answer was two. Your drink.”

Once again, Vervain was back-petaling. Her coat fluttered in surprise and disorientation, but she obediently dipped a root-tendril into one of the concoctions she’d settled upon the console.

Affini intoxicants took on many forms. Vervain’s particular solution was a tyrosine-based slurry that her body could break down into dopamine, combined with an herbicidal compound. The latter would inhibit the efficiency of her photosynthesis and offset the effect of the dopamine.

All of which is to say it would get her more-or-less drunk.

“… So, you believe in our goal? That’s not what you’ve led us to believe,” Vervain remarked as the slurry blurred into her system. Wow, that’s strong. Okay. Easy does it, you can handle this Vervain.

“You’ve never asked what I believe,” Elize announced smugly. “I told you I didn’t want to be one of your little pets. I didn’t say humanity didn’t deserve it. I mean, look around; we devoured our home planet and ruined it, then started moving elsewhere. The rich get richer while a bunch of us idiots just keep dying for their causes. All-in-all, a life of luxury at the cost of being some plant’s bitch once in a while doesn’t seem so bad.”

’Don’t you think toying with her like this is a little cruel, Elize?’ Poppy prompted. The way that Vervain was reacting made it clear that she was embarrassed, but, well… Elize was enjoying herself. Honestly, this was more fun than she’d had in a while.

’Oh, just let me have this for a bit, floret.’ Critical hit. Poppy was out-of-commission until she stopped being flustered over the notion of being called such a thing, so Elize could have some fun in the meantime.

And then give her even more abandonment issues?

Oof.

Vervain drank. She felt a little bad for her particular lie, but she was having trouble thinking clearly, and Elize kept saying fascinating things while continuing to be incredibly distracting.

The drink boiled across her body and made her feel warm and giddy. In a way, Elize was right. She was finding it easier to relax.

Vervain struggled. There was something itching at the corner of her consciousness, something she couldn’t quite reach through the haze of chemicals in her system.
